可他是安全的。脚趾、鼻子和脸颊不过让霜轻轻碰一碰罢了，火已经烧出劲道来了。他正往里添手指粗细的枝条。再过一分钟，他就能添手腕粗细的树枝了，到那时他就可以脱掉湿透的鞋袜，一面烤着它们，一面光着脚在火边取暖——当然，得先拿雪搓一搓脚。这火生成了。他安全了。他想起硫磺溪那位老前辈的忠告，笑了。老前辈板着脸立下过规矩：零下五十度以后，在克朗代克（Klondike），谁也不许独自赶路。瞧，他这不是好好的；出了岔子；就他孤身一个；可他把自己救了。那些老前辈啊，有几个未免太婆妈，他想。一个人只要脑子放得稳，就出不了事。是个男子汉的，就能独自赶路。只是叫人吃惊的是，他的脸颊和鼻子冻结得竟这样快。他也没想到，手指竟会在这么短的工夫里失了生气。它们确是没了生气，因为他几乎没法叫它们一齐使劲去攥一根细枝，它们仿佛离他的身子很远，离他这个人很远。碰着一根细枝的时候，他得用眼睛看，才知道自己抓住了没有。他和他指尖之间的线路，已经断得差不多了。

===Original===

All of which counted for little. There was the fire, snapping and crackling and promising life with every dancing flame. He started to untie his moccasins. They were coated with ice; the thick German socks were like sheaths of iron halfway to the knees; and the mocassin strings were like rods of steel all twisted and knotted as by some conflagration. For a moment he tugged with his numbed fingers, then, realizing the folly of it, he drew his sheath-knife.

But before he could cut the strings, it happened. It was his own fault or, rather, his mistake. He should not have built the fire under the spruce tree. He should have built it in the open. But it had been easier to pull the twigs from the brush and drop them directly on the fire. Now the tree under which he had done this carried a weight of snow on its boughs. No wind had blown for weeks, and each bough was fully freighted. Each time he had pulled a twig he had communicated a slight agitation to the tree﻿—an imperceptible agitation, so far as he was concerned, but an agitation sufficient to bring about the disaster. High up in the tree one bough capsized its load of snow. This fell on the boughs beneath, capsizing them. This process continued, spreading out and involving the whole tree. It grew like an avalanche, and it descended without warning upon the man and the fire, and the fire was blotted out! Where it had burned was a mantle of fresh and disordered snow.

The man was shocked. It was as though he had just heard his own sentence of death. For a moment he sat and stared at the spot where the fire had been. Then he grew very calm. Perhaps the old-timer on Sulphur Creek was right. If he had only had a trail-mate he would have been in no danger now. The trail-mate could have built the fire. Well, it was up to him to build the fire over again, and this second time there must be no failure. Even if he succeeded, he would most likely lose some toes. His feet must be badly frozen by now, and there would be some time before the second fire was ready.

===Chinese===

这一切全算不得什么。火就在那儿，噼噼啪啪地响，每一下跳动的火苗都在许诺着生机。他动手去解鹿皮鞋。鞋上裹满了冰；厚厚的德国绒袜到膝盖一半硬得像铁鞘；鞋带像一根根钢条，绞结成一团，仿佛遭过一场大火。他用冻麻的手指拽了片刻，随即醒悟到这是犯傻，便抽出了鞘刀。

可还没等他割断鞋带，事情就发生了。这得怪他自己，或者不如说，怪他失算。他不该把火生在云杉树底下。他该生在空地上。可从灌木丛里抽出细枝，径直丢进火里，要省事得多。此刻，他这么做了的那棵树，枝头上正驮着满满的积雪。几个星期没刮过一丝风，每一根枝条都载得满满当当。他每抽一根细枝，就传给树身一次轻微的震颤——在他这方面，那震颤轻得无从察觉，可要酿成这场灾祸却绰绰有余。树的高处，一根枝条翻卸下了它驮着的雪。雪落在底下的枝条上，把那些枝条也压翻了。这个过程接连不断，扩散开来，牵动了整棵树。它像一场雪崩，越滚越大，毫无征兆地扑向那人和火堆，火被扑灭了！方才燃烧的地方，盖上了一层新落下的、乱糟糟的雪。

那人惊呆了。这就像他刚听见对自己死刑的宣判。他坐着，呆呆地盯了一会儿火堆原先所在的地方。随后他变得非常镇定。硫磺溪的那位老前辈也许是对的。当初要是有个同路的伴儿，他这会儿就没有危险了。同伴可以把火生起来。唉，如今只好靠他自己从头再生一堆火了，而这第二回，万万失不得手。就算成了，他也多半要丢掉几个脚趾。他的脚这会儿一定冻得不轻，而第二堆火生好之前，还得熬上一阵子。
===Original===

Such were his thoughts, but he did not sit and think them. He was busy all the time they were passing through his mind, he made a new foundation for a fire, this time in the open; where no treacherous tree could blot it out. Next, he gathered dry grasses and tiny twigs from the high-water flotsam. He could not bring his fingers together to pull them out, but he was able to gather them by the handful. In this way he got many rotten twigs and bits of green moss that were undesirable, but it was the best he could do. He worked methodically, even collecting an armful of the larger branches to be used later when the fire gathered strength. And all the while the dog sat and watched him, a certain yearning wistfulness in its eyes, for it looked upon him as the fire-provider, and the fire was slow in coming.

When all was ready, the man reached in his pocket for a second piece of birch-bark. He knew the bark was there, and, though he could not feel it with his fingers, he could hear its crisp rustling as he fumbled for it. Try as he would, he could not clutch hold of it. And all the time, in his consciousness, was the knowledge that each instant his feet were freezing. This thought tended to put him in a panic, but he fought against it and kept calm. He pulled on his mittens with his teeth, and threshed his arms back and forth, beating his hands with all his might against his sides. He did this sitting down, and he stood up to do it; and all the while the dog sat in the snow, its wolf-brush of a tail curled around warmly over its forefeet, its sharp wolf-ears pricked forward intently as it watched the man. And the man as he beat and threshed with his arms and hands, felt a great surge of envy as he regarded the creature that was warm and secure in its natural covering.

After a time he was aware of the first faraway signals of sensation in his beaten fingers. The faint tingling grew stronger till it evolved into a stinging ache that was excruciating, but which the man hailed with satisfaction. He stripped the mitten from his right hand and fetched forth the birch-bark. The exposed fingers were quickly going numb again. Next he brought out his bunch of sulphur matches. But the tremendous cold had already driven the life out of his fingers. In his effort to separate one match from the others, the whole bunch fell in the snow. He tried to pick it out of the snow, but failed. The dead fingers could neither touch nor clutch. He was very careful. He drove the thought of his freezing feet; and nose, and cheeks, out of his mind, devoting his whole soul to the matches. He watched, using the sense of vision in place of that of touch, and when he saw his fingers on each side the bunch, he closed them﻿—that is, he willed to close them, for the wires were drawn, and the fingers did not obey. He pulled the mitten on the right hand, and beat it fiercely against his knee. Then, with both mittened hands, he scooped the bunch of matches, along with much snow, into his lap. Yet he was no better off.

===Chinese===

他心里转着这些念头，人可没有坐着干想。这些念头在脑子里过的时候，他手底下一直忙个不停：他给新火堆打新的底子，这回打在空地上，再不会有哪棵居心叵测的树来扑灭它了。接着，他从春汛的漂浮物里收拢干草和细枝。他的手指合不拢，没法把它们一根根抽出来，只能一把一把地捧。这样捧来的东西里，混着不少不该要的朽枝和青苔碎块，可他也只能做到这一步了。他干得有条不紊，甚至还搂了一抱较粗的树枝，预备等火势旺起来之后再添。这当儿，那条狗一直坐在旁边望着他，眼睛里带着一种渴望的、有所期待的神情，因为它把他看作供火的人，而火迟迟不来。

一切就绪，那人伸手到口袋里去掏第二片桦树皮。他知道树皮就在那儿，手指虽然摸不出来，摸索之间却听得见它脆脆的窸窣声。他怎么试也攥不住它。而自始至终，他的意识里都横着一个明白：他的脚正在一分一秒地冻下去。这个念头眼看要叫他慌乱，可他抵住它，保持着镇定。他用牙齿把手套扯上去，来回抡着胳膊，使出全身的劲儿把双手往两肋上捶。他坐着捶过，又站起来捶；这中间，狗一直蹲在雪里，那条狼刷子似的大尾巴暖暖地蜷起来盖住前爪，一对尖尖的狼耳朵专注地朝前竖着，望着那人。那人一边捶打着、抡着胳膊和手，一边望着那畜生——它裹着天生的皮毛，又暖和，又安稳——心里涌起一阵大大的艳羡。

过了一阵，他觉出那双挨了捶的手指有了知觉的最初、最遥远的信号。微弱的麻刺越来越强，最后变成一阵钻心的刺痛，可那人迎接它，满怀欢喜。他褪下右手的连指手套，取出那片桦树皮。裸露的手指很快又麻了。接着他掏出那一小把硫磺火柴。可那骇人的严寒早已把手指里的生命赶了出去。他想从火柴束里分出一根来，整把火柴却掉进了雪里。他想把它从雪里捡起来，没成功。死去的手指既碰不着，也攥不拢。他小心极了。他把脚在冻、鼻子在冻、脸颊在冻这些念头统统赶出脑子，把整个魂儿都扑在火柴上。他用眼睛代替触觉盯着看，看见手指落到了火柴束的两侧，便合拢它们——就是说，他命令它们合拢，因为线路已经断了，手指不听调遣。他给右手戴上手套，狠狠地在膝盖上捶。然后，他用两只戴手套的手，把那把火柴连同大量的雪一起捧进了怀里。可他的处境并没有好一点儿。

===Original===

After some manipulation he managed to get the bunch between the heels of his mittened hands. In this fashion he carried it to his mouth. The ice crackled and snapped when by a violent effort he opened his mouth. He drew the lower jaw in, curled the upper lip out of the way, and scraped the bunch with his upper teeth in order to separate a match. He succeeded in getting one, which he dropped on his lap. He was no better off. He could not pick it up. Then he devised a way. He picked it up in his teeth and scratched it on his leg. Twenty times he scratched before he succeeded in lighting it. As it flamed he held it with his teeth to the birch-bark. But the burning brimstone went up his nostrils and into his lungs, causing him to cough spasmodically. The match fell into the snow and went out.

The old-timer on Sulphur Creek was right, he thought in the moment of controlled despair that ensued: after fifty below, a man should travel with a partner. He beat his hands, but failed in exciting any sensation. Suddenly he bared both hands, removing the mittens with his teeth. He caught the whole bunch between the heels of his hands. His arm-muscles not being frozen enabled him to press the hand-heels tightly against the matches. Then he scratched the bunch along his leg. It flared into flame, seventy sulphur matches at once! There was no wind to blow them out. He kept his head to one side to escape the strangling fumes, and held the blazing bunch to the birch-bark. As he so held it, he became aware of sensation in his hand. His flesh was burning. He could smell it. Deep down below the surface he could feel it. The sensation developed into pain that grew acute. And still he endured it, holding the flame of the matches clumsily to the bark that would not light readily because his own burning hands were in the way, absorbing most of the flame.

At last, when he could endure no more, he jerked his hands apart. The blazing matches fell sizzling into the snow, but the birch-bark was alight. He began laying dry grasses and the tiniest twigs on the flame. He could not pick and choose, for he had to lift the fuel between the heels of his hands. Small pieces of rotten wood and green moss clung to the twigs, and he bit them off as well as he could with his teeth. He cherished the flame carefully and awkwardly. It meant life, and it must not perish. The withdrawal of blood from the surface of his body now made him begin to shiver, and he grew more awkward. A large piece of green moss fell squarely on the little fire. He tried to poke it out with his fingers, but his shivering frame made him poke too far, and he disrupted the nucleus of the little fire, the burning grasses and tiny twigs separating and scattering. He tried to poke them together again, but in spite of the tenseness of the effort, his shivering got away with him, and the twigs were hopelessly scattered. Each twig gushed a puff of smoke and went out. The fire-provider had failed. As he looked apathetically about him, his eyes chanced on the dog, sitting across the ruins of the fire from him, in the snow, making restless, hunching movements, slightly lifting one forefoot and then the other, shifting its weight back and forth on them with wistful eagerness.

===Chinese===

摆弄了一阵，他总算把那把火柴夹在了两只戴手套的手的掌根中间。他就这么把它凑到嘴边。他狠命一使劲张开嘴，冰噼里啪啦地炸响。他收进下颚，把上嘴唇卷开让路，用上牙刮那把火柴，想刮下一根来。他成功弄下了一根，掉在他怀里。他的处境并没有好一点儿。他捡不起它来。于是他想出一个法子。他用牙齿把它衔起来，往腿上划。划了二十下，才划着了。火苗一起，他用牙齿衔着它去凑桦树皮。可燃烧的硫磺直钻他的鼻孔，灌进他的肺，呛得他一阵阵痉挛地咳嗽。火柴掉进雪里，灭了。

硫磺溪的那位老前辈是对的——在接下来那个强自克制着的绝望时刻，他这样想：零下五十度以后，人该带着伙伴赶路。他捶打双手，却唤不起半点知觉。突然他亮出双手，用牙齿褪掉了手套。他把整把火柴夹在两只手的掌根中间。胳膊上的肌肉还没有冻住，还能叫他把掌根紧紧压在火柴上。然后他把那把火柴往腿上划。呼啦一下全着了，七十根硫磺火柴一齐烧起来！没有风来吹熄它们。他把头偏向一侧，躲开那股呛死人的烟气，把烧得正旺的一把火柴凑向桦树皮。凑着的时候，他觉出手上有了知觉。他的肉在烧。他闻得见。皮肉底下的深处，他感觉得到。那知觉变成了疼，疼得越来越尖锐。他仍旧忍着，把火柴的火焰笨拙地抵在树皮上——树皮不容易着，因为他自己烧着的手正挡在中间，吸走了大半的火苗。

终于，忍到再也忍不住，他把两手猛地一扯。烧着的火柴嘶嘶响着落进雪里，可桦树皮着了。他开始往火苗上铺干草和顶细的枝条。他没法挑挑拣拣，因为燃料只能夹在两个掌根中间搬运。细枝上粘着朽木碎块和青苔，他只能尽量用牙齿去咬掉。他小心翼翼又笨手笨脚地呵护着火苗。它意味着命，绝不能让它灭了。血液从身体表面撤退，这时叫他打起了寒战，动作越发笨拙。一大块青苔不偏不倚落在小火堆正中。他想用手指把它拨开，可打着颤的身子叫他拨过了头，把小火堆的核心捣散了，烧着的干草和细枝分了家，四散开去。他想再把它们拨拢，可任凭他怎么绷紧了劲，寒战还是制服了他，细枝散得没了指望。每一根细枝喷出一股烟，灭了。供火的人失手了。他神情木然地朝四下里张望，目光落在那条狗身上——狗坐在火堆残迹的对面，蹲在雪里，不安地拱动着身子，这只前爪微微抬一下，那只前爪再抬一下，把重心在两只爪子间急切地挪来挪去。

===Original===

The sight of the dog put a wild idea into his head. He remembered the tale of the man, caught in a blizzard, who killed a steer and crawled inside the carcass, and so was saved. He would kill the dog and bury his hands in the warm body until the numbness went out of them. Then he could build another fire. He spoke to the dog, calling it to him; but in his voice was a strange note of fear that frightened the animal, who had never known the man to speak in such way before. Something was the matter, and its suspicious nature sensed danger﻿—it knew not what danger but somewhere, somehow, in its brain arose an apprehension of the man. It flattened its ears down at the sound of the man’s voice, and its restless, hunching movements and the liftings and shiftings of its forefeet became more pronounced but it would not come to the man. He got on his hands and knees and crawled toward the dog. This unusual posture again excited suspicion, and the animal sidled mincingly away.

The man sat up in the snow for a moment and struggled for calmness. Then he pulled on his mittens, by means of his teeth, and got upon his feet. He glanced down at first in order to assure himself that he was really standing up, for the absence of sensation in his feet left him unrelated to the earth. His erect position in itself started to drive the webs of suspicion from the dog’s mind; and when he spoke peremptorily, with the sound of whiplashes in his voice, the dog rendered its customary allegiance and came to him. As it came within reaching distance, the man lost his control. His arms flashed out to the dog, and he experienced genuine surprise when he discovered that his hands could not clutch, that there was neither bend nor feeling in the fingers. He had forgotten for the moment that they were frozen and that they were freezing more and more. All this happened quickly, and before the animal could get away, he encircled its body with his arms. He sat down in the snow, and in this fashion held the dog, while it snarled and whined and struggled.

But it was all he could do, hold its body encircled in his arms and sit there. He realized that he could not kill the dog. There was no way to do it. With his helpless hands he could neither draw nor hold his sheath-knife nor throttle the animal. He released it, and it plunged wildly away, with tail between its legs, and still snarling. It halted forty feet away and surveyed him curiously, with ears sharply pricked forward. The man looked down at his hands in order to locate them, and found them hanging on the ends of his arms. It struck him as curious that one should have to use his eyes in order to find out where his hands were. He began threshing his arms back and forth, beating the mittened hands against his sides. He did this for five minutes, violently, and his heart pumped enough blood up to the surface to put a stop to his shivering. But no sensation was aroused in the hands. He had an impression that they hung like weights on the ends of his arms, but when he tried to run the impression down, he could not find it.

===Chinese===

看见这条狗，他脑子里冒出一个疯狂的念头。他想起一个故事：有个人让暴风雪困住，宰了一头阉牛，钻进牛尸里，就这样保住了性命。他要杀了这条狗，把两只手埋进那温热的躯体，直到麻木从手里褪出去。然后他就能再生一堆火。他开口唤狗，叫它过来；可他的声音里带着一种奇怪的惧意，把那畜生吓着了——它从没听这人用这样的腔调说过话。出事了，它多疑的天性嗅出了危险——什么危险它不知道，可不知在哪儿、不知怎么的，它脑子里对这个人升起了一股畏惧。听见那人的声音，它把耳朵贴伏下去，那不安的拱动、前爪的抬起和挪换愈发明显，可它不肯到那人跟前去。那人手脚并用地趴下，朝狗爬过去。这不同寻常的姿势又勾起了它的疑心，那畜生迈着小碎步，侧着身子躲开了。

那人在雪里坐起身来，挣扎着定了定神。然后他用牙齿把手套扯上去，站了起来。他先低头看了一眼，好确信自己真的站起来了，因为双脚没了知觉，他和大地已经断了关联。他直立的姿态本身就开始把狗心里的疑团驱散了；等他厉声呵斥，嗓音里带着鞭子的声调，狗便照例表示了顺从，朝他走过来。等它走进够得着的距离，那人失控了。他的双臂猛地朝狗甩出去，随即他当真吃了一惊——他发现双手攥不拢了，手指既弯不动，也没有知觉。他一时忘了它们是冻了的，而且正越冻越深。这一切来得飞快，那畜生还没来得及逃开，他已用两条胳膊圈住了它的身子。他坐倒在雪里，就这样抱着狗，狗在他怀里咆哮、哀叫、挣扎。

可他所能做的，也只有这些了：用胳膊圈着狗的身子，坐在那儿。他明白自己杀不了这条狗。没有法子下手。凭这双无用的手，他既拔不出也握不住鞘刀，更掐不死这畜生。他松开手，狗夹着尾巴疯了一样蹿出去，一路还在咆哮。它在四十英尺开外站住，好奇地打量他，尖耳朵警觉地朝前竖着。那人低头去看自己的手，好确定它们在哪儿，却发现它们坠在胳膊的末梢上。一个人竟要用眼睛才弄得清自己的手在哪儿，这事想来也怪。他开始来回抡胳膊，把戴手套的手往两肋上捶。他狠狠捶了五分钟，心脏泵上来足够的血，止住了寒战。可手上还是唤不起一丝知觉。他有个印象，觉得它们像秤锤一样坠在胳膊梢上，可等他想追索这个印象，却又找它不着了。
===Original===

A certain fear of death, dull and oppressive, came to him. This fear quickly became poignant as he realized that it was no longer a mere matter of freezing his fingers and toes, or of losing his hands and feet, but that it was a matter of life and death with the chances against him. This threw him into a panic, and he turned and ran up the creek-bed along the old, dim trail. The dog joined in behind and kept up with him. He ran blindly, without intention, in fear such as he had never known in his life. Slowly, as he ploughed and floundered through the snow, he began to see things again﻿—the banks of the creek, the old timber-jams, the leafless aspens, and the sky. The running made him feel better. He did not shiver. Maybe, if he ran on, his feet would thaw out; and, anyway, if he ran far enough, he would reach camp and the boys. Without doubt he would lose some fingers and toes and some of his face; but the boys would take care of him, and save the rest of him when he got there. And at the same time there was another thought in his mind that said he would never get to the camp and the boys; that it was too many miles away, that the freezing had too great a start on him, and that he would soon be stiff and dead. This thought he kept in the background and refused to consider. Sometimes it pushed itself forward and demanded to be heard, but he thrust it back and strove to think of other things.

It struck him as curious that he could run at all on feet so frozen that he could not feel them when they struck the earth and took the weight of his body. He seemed to himself to skim along above the surface and to have no connection with the earth. Somewhere he had once seen a winged Mercury, and he wondered if Mercury felt as he felt when skimming over the earth.

His theory of running until he reached camp and the boys had one flaw in it: he lacked the endurance. Several times he stumbled, and finally he tottered, crumpled up, and fell. When he tried to rise, he failed. He must sit and rest, he decided, and next time he would merely walk and keep on going. As he sat and regained his breath, he noted that he was feeling quite warm and comfortable. He was not shivering, and it even seemed that a warm glow had come to his chest and trunk. And yet, when he touched his nose or cheeks, there was no sensation. Running would not thaw them out. Nor would it thaw out his hands and feet. Then the thought came to him that the frozen portions of his body must be extending. He tried to keep this thought down, to forget it, to think of something else; he was aware of the panicky feeling that it caused, and he was afraid of the panic. But the thought asserted itself, and persisted, until it produced a vision of his body totally frozen. This was too much, and he made another wild run along the trail. Once he slowed down to a walk, but the thought of the freezing extending itself made him run again.

===Chinese===

一种对死亡的恐惧，沉沉的、压人的，落到了他身上。他随即明白过来：这已不只是冻掉几个手指脚趾、丢掉两手两脚的事，而是一场生死之争，而且胜算不在他这边。恐惧顿时变得尖锐。这把他推进了慌乱，他转身沿着那条依稀的旧道，往溪床上游跑去。狗跟在后面，寸步不离。他跑得盲目，没有意图，一身他这辈子从未尝过的恐惧。他在雪里连蹚带扑腾，慢慢地，又看得见东西了——溪的两岸、壅塞的老木头、光秃秃的山杨，还有天空。跑着跑着，他觉得好受些了。他不打寒战了。也许接着跑下去，脚能缓过来；再说了，只要跑得够远，总能到营地，到伙伴们那儿。毫无疑问，他会丢掉几个手指脚趾，脸上也得丢掉几块；可伙伴们会照料他，等他到了，会把他剩下的部分救下来。与此同时，他脑子里还有一个念头，说他永远到不了营地、见不着伙伴们；说路太远，冻结抢在他前头太多，他很快就要僵了、死了。这个念头，他把它压在背后，拒绝去想。有时它自己往前拱，嚷着要人听，可他把它推回去，拼命想些别的。

他暗暗觉得奇怪：双脚冻得那样厉害，踩到地上、承起他身子的分量时竟毫无知觉，可他居然还能跑。他仿佛觉得自己贴着地面飘掠而行，跟大地毫无牵连。他记得在哪儿见过一尊长翅膀的墨丘利（Mercury），他纳闷，墨丘利掠过大地的时候，是不是也和他此刻一个滋味。

他那个跑到营地、跑到伙伴们跟前的打算，里头有一个破绽：他缺这份耐力。他绊了好几跤，终于摇晃起来，瘫软下去，倒下了。他想爬起来，爬不起来。得坐下歇歇，他打定主意，下一回只消走着去，别停就行。他坐着，缓过气来，发觉自己竟觉得相当暖和、相当舒服。他不打寒战，胸口和躯干里甚至像漫起了一股暖融融的热。可是，摸摸鼻子，摸摸脸颊，毫无知觉。跑是暖不回它们了。手和脚也一样暖不回来。跟着，一个念头来了：他身上冻住的部分一定正在扩展。他想压住这个念头，想忘掉它，想点别的；他觉出了它引起的那阵慌乱，他怕这慌乱。可这念头自己站定了，赖着不走，直到逼出一幅图景：他全身彻底冻结。这他受不住，便顺着小路又撒腿狂奔起来。有一回他放慢成了走，可一想到那不断扩展的冻结，又跑了起来。

===Original===

And all the time the dog ran with him, at his heels. When he fell down a second time, it curled its tail over its forefeet and sat in front of him facing him curiously eager and intent. The warmth and security of the animal angered him, and he cursed it till it flattened down its ears appeasingly. This time the shivering came more quickly upon the man. He was losing in his battle with the frost. It was creeping into his body from all sides. The thought of it drove him on, but he ran no more than a hundred feet, when he staggered and pitched headlong. It was his last panic. When he had recovered his breath and control, he sat up and entertained in his mind the conception of meeting death with dignity. However, the conception did not come to him in such terms. His idea of it was that he had been making a fool of himself, running around like a chicken with its head cut off﻿—such was the simile that occurred to him. Well, he was bound to freeze anyway, and he might as well take it decently. With this newfound peace of mind came the first glimmerings of drowsiness. A good idea, he thought, to sleep off to death. It was like taking an anaesthetic. Freezing was not so bad as people thought. There were lots worse ways to die.

He pictured the boys finding his body next day. Suddenly he found himself with them, coming along the trail and looking for himself. And, still with them, he came around a turn in the trail and found himself lying in the snow. He did not belong with himself any more, for even then he was out of himself, standing with the boys and looking at himself in the snow. It certainly was cold, was his thought. When he got back to the States he could tell the folks what real cold was. He drifted on from this to a vision of the old-timer on Sulphur Creek. He could see him quite clearly, warm and comfortable, and smoking a pipe.

“You were right, old hoss; you were right,” the man mumbled to the old-timer of Sulphur Creek.

===Chinese===

这一路，狗始终跟着他，缀在他脚跟后面。他第二回倒下时，它把尾巴蜷来盖住前爪，坐在他对面，好奇而急切地盯着他。这畜生的温暖和安稳惹恼了他，他咒骂它，直骂得它讨饶似的把耳朵贴伏下去。这一回，寒战更快地落到了那人身上。他在同严寒的搏斗里正在落败。寒气正从四面八方往他身子里面渗。这个念头驱赶着他，可他跑了不过一百英尺，就踉踉跄跄，一头栽了下去。这是他最后一场慌乱。等他喘过气、定住神，他坐起身来，心里转着一个念头：要死得体面些。不过，这念头到他心里，并不是这样的字眼。他自己对它的想法是：他方才一直像个傻子，像只砍了头的鸡似的到处乱窜——他脑子里冒出来的就是这个比方。唉，横竖是要冻死的，不如死得像样点。随着这份新得的心安，最初的睡意悄悄地泛起。好主意，他想，一觉睡到死。就像打一针麻药。冻死并不像人们想的那么糟。比这糟的死法有的是。

他想象着伙伴们第二天找到他尸首的情景。忽然间，他发现自己已经和他们在一起，顺着小路走来，找他自己。他还和他们在一处，绕过小路的一个拐弯，看见自己躺在雪里。他不再属于自己的身子了，因为就在那时，他已脱出了自身，和伙伴们站在一起，望着雪里的自己。这天可真冷，他想。等回到美国本土（the States），他可以把真正的冷讲给乡亲们听。想着想着，他飘到了硫磺溪那位老前辈的幻影跟前。他把那人看得很清楚，暖暖和和，舒舒服服，正抽着一斗烟。

“你说得对，老伙计；你说得对。”那人朝着硫磺溪那位老前辈喃喃地说。

===Original===

Then the man drowsed off into what seemed to him the most comfortable and satisfying sleep he had ever known. The dog sat facing him and waiting. The brief day drew to a close in a long, slow twilight. There were no signs of a fire to be made, and, besides, never in the dog’s experience had it known a man to sit like that in the snow and make no fire. As the twilight drew on, its eager yearning for the fire mastered it, and with a great lifting and shifting of forefeet, it whined softly, then flattened its ears down in anticipation of being chidden by the man. But the man remained silent. Later, the dog whined loudly. And still later it crept close to the man and caught the scent of death. This made the animal bristle and back away. A little longer it delayed, howling under the stars that leaped and danced and shone brightly in the cold sky. Then it turned and trotted up the trail in the direction of the camp it knew, where were the other food-providers and fire-providers.

===Chinese===

随后，那人打起盹来，沉入他平生所知最舒服、最餍足的一觉。狗坐在他对面，等着。短促的白昼在一场漫长而缓慢的暮色里合上了。没有一点儿要生火的迹象，再说，在狗的全部阅历里，它也从没见过一个人这样坐在雪里不生火。暮色渐浓，它对火的渴望压倒了一切，它把前爪抬了又换、换了又抬，呜呜地低叫，随即又把耳朵贴伏下去，预备挨那人的呵斥。可那人一声不吭。后来，狗大声呜咽起来。再后来，它爬到那人跟前，嗅到了死亡的气味。这气味炸起了它一身的毛，逼得它直往后退。它又捱了一会儿，在寒空里那些跳跃、舞动、灼灼闪亮的星星底下嗥叫。然后它转过身，顺着小路，朝它认得的那个营地小跑而去——那里有别的一些供食的人、供火的人。

## Trust / 信任

===Original===

All lines had been cast off, and the *Seattle No. 4* was pulling slowly out from the shore. Her decks were piled high with freight and baggage, and swarmed with a heterogeneous company of Indians, dogs, and dog-mushers, prospectors, traders, and homeward-bound gold-seekers. A goodly portion of Dawson was lined up on the bank, saying goodbye. As the gangplank came in and the steamer nosed into the stream, the clamour of farewell became deafening. Also, in that eleventh moment, everybody began to remember final farewell messages and to shout them back and forth across the widening stretch of water. Louis Bondell, curling his yellow moustache with one hand and languidly waving the other hand to his friends on shore, suddenly remembered something and sprang to the rail.

“Oh, Fred!” he bawled. “Oh, Fred!”

The “Fred” desired thrust a strapping pair of shoulders through the forefront of the crowd on the bank and tried to catch Louis Bondell’s message. The latter grew red in the face with vain vociferation. Still the water widened between steamboat and shore.

“Hey, you, Captain Scott!” he yelled at the pilothouse. “Stop the boat!”

===Chinese===

缆绳全都解开了，「西雅图四号」（*Seattle No. 4*）正缓缓离岸。甲板上货运行李堆得老高，挤着形形色色的一群：印第安人、狗，还有赶狗橇的、探矿的、做买卖的，和返乡的淘金客。道森（Dawson）城一多半人都排在岸上道别。跳板一收，轮船朝河心拱去，道别的喧声顿时震耳欲聋。也就在这最后的时刻，人人都想起了临别的叮嘱，隔着越拉越宽的水面来回喊话。路易斯·邦德尔（Louis Bondell）一只手捻着他的黄胡子，另一只手懒洋洋地朝岸上的朋友们挥着，忽然想起了什么，一个箭步蹿到栏杆边。

“喂，弗雷德！”他扯开嗓子喊。“喂，弗雷德！”

被喊的那位“弗雷德”从岸边人群的最前排挤出一副魁伟的肩膀，想接住路易斯·邦德尔的话。后者白喊了半天，脸都涨红了。轮船和岸之间的水面还在继续加宽。

“嘿，说你呢，斯科特船长（Captain Scott）！”他朝驾驶室吼道。“把船停一停！”

===Original===

The gongs clanged, and the big stern wheel reversed, then stopped. All hands on steamboat and on bank took advantage of this respite to exchange final, new, and imperative farewells. More futile than ever was Louis Bondell’s effort to make himself heard. The *Seattle No. 4* lost way and drifted downstream, and Captain Scott had to go ahead and reverse a second time. His head disappeared inside the pilothouse, coming into view a moment later behind a big megaphone.

Now Captain Scott had a remarkable voice, and the “Shut up!” he launched at the crowd on deck and on shore could have been heard at the top of Moosehide Mountain and as far as Klondike City. This official remonstrance from the pilothouse spread a film of silence over the tumult.

“Now, what do you want to say?” Captain Scott demanded.

“Tell Fred Churchill﻿—he’s on the bank there﻿—tell him to go to Macdonald. It’s in his safe﻿—a small gripsack of mine. Tell him to get it and bring it out when he comes.”

In the silence Captain Scott bellowed the message ashore through the megaphone:﻿—

“You, Fred Churchill, go to Macdonald﻿—in his safe﻿—small gripsack﻿—belongs to Louis Bondell﻿—important! Bring it out when you come! Got it!”

===Chinese===

铜锣当当地敲响，巨大的尾明轮倒转起来，随即停住。船上和岸上的所有人都趁着这个间隙，交换起最后的、新添的、非交代不可的道别。路易斯·邦德尔想让人听见自己，这番力气比先前更是白费。「西雅图四号」失去了冲力，顺水往下游漂，斯科特船长只好再开船，第二回倒车。他的脑袋缩进驾驶室不见了，片刻之后，出现在一只大扩音喇叭后面。

斯科特船长天生一副惊人的嗓门，他朝甲板上和岸上人群抛去的那声“住口！”，穆斯海德山（Moosehide Mountain）顶上都听得见，一直传到克朗代克城（Klondike City）。驾驶室里发出的这声官腔训诫，给满场喧闹蒙上了一层薄薄的寂静。

“好了，你要说什么？”斯科特船长问。

“告诉弗雷德·丘吉尔（Fred Churchill）——他就在岸上——叫他去麦克唐纳（Macdonald）那儿。东西在他的保险柜里——我的一只小提包。叫他去取出来，等他动身的时候带出来。”

寂静中，斯科特船长对着喇叭，把这口信吼上了岸：——

“你，弗雷德·丘吉尔，去麦克唐纳那儿——保险柜里——小提包——路易斯·邦德尔的——要紧东西！来的时候带出来！听明白了！”

===Original===

Churchill waved his hand in token that he had got it. In truth, had Macdonald, half a mile away, opened his window, he’d have got it, too. The tumult of farewell rose again, the gongs clanged, and the *Seattle No. 4* went ahead, swung out into the stream, turned on her heel, and headed down the Yukon, Bondell and Churchill waving farewell and mutual affection to the last.

That was in midsummer. In the fall of the year, the *W. H. Willis* started up the Yukon with two hundred homeward-bound pilgrims on board. Among them was Churchill. In his stateroom, in the middle of a clothes-bag, was Louis Bondell’s grip. It was a small, stout leather affair, and its weight of forty pounds always made Churchill nervous when he wandered too far from it. The man in the adjoining stateroom had a treasure of gold-dust hidden similarly in a clothes-bag, and the pair of them ultimately arranged to stand watch and watch. While one went down to eat, the other kept an eye on the two stateroom doors. When Churchill wanted to take a hand at whist, the other man mounted guard, and when the other man wanted to relax his soul, Churchill read four-months’ old newspapers on a camp stool between the two doors.

There were signs of an early winter, and the question that was discussed from dawn till dark, and far into the dark, was whether they would get out before the freeze-up or be compelled to abandon the steamboat and tramp out over the ice. There were irritating delays. Twice the engines broke down and had to be tinkered up, and each time there were snow flurries to warn them of the imminence of winter. Nine times the *W. H. Willis* essayed to ascend the Five-Finger Rapids with her impaired machinery, and when she succeeded, she was four days behind her very liberal schedule. The question that then arose was whether or not the steamboat *Flora* would wait for her above the Box Canyon. The stretch of water between the head of the Box Canyon and the foot of the White Horse Rapids was unnavigable for steamboats, and passengers were transhipped at that point, walking around the rapids from one steamboat to the other. There were no telephones in the country, hence no way of informing the waiting *Flora* that the *Willis* was four days late, but coming.

===Chinese===

丘吉尔挥手示意听明白了。说实话，哪怕半英里外的麦克唐纳推开窗户，他也能听明白。道别的喧声重新涨起来，铜锣当当，「西雅图四号」开足马力，摆进河心，原地掉了个头，顺育空河（Yukon）往下游开去，邦德尔和丘吉尔彼此挥手道别、情谊绵绵，直到最后一眼。

这是仲夏时节的事。到了那年秋天，「W. H. 威利斯号」（*W. H. Willis*）载着两百名返乡的淘金客驶上育空河。丘吉尔也在其中。他的舱房里，一只衣袋的正当中，放着路易斯·邦德尔的提包。那是件小巧结实的皮革物件，四十磅的分量，害得丘吉尔每逢离它太远，心里就发紧。隔壁舱房的那一位，也同样把一份沙金藏在衣袋当中，两人末了商定轮流守望。一个下楼吃饭，另一个就盯住两间舱房的门。丘吉尔想上场打一把惠斯特牌（whist）的时候，那人便替他站岗；那人想松快松快心神的时候，丘吉尔就坐在两门中间的小折凳上，读四个月前的旧报纸。

种种迹象显示，这年的冬天来得早。从天亮到天黑，又从天黑到深夜，大家翻来覆去议论的都是一件事：船究竟能不能赶在封冻前出去，还是只得扔下轮船，踏着冰面徒步走出来。耽搁一场接着一场，恼人得很。机器坏了两回，都只得敲敲打打修修补补，每回都飘起雪来，警告他们冬天已经迫在眉睫。「W. H. 威利斯号」拖着她那带伤的机器，一连九次尝试冲上五指急流（Five-Finger Rapids），等她冲过去了，已经比那份宽打宽算的船期表晚了四天。随之而来的问题是：「弗洛拉号」（*Flora*）轮船究竟还肯不肯在博克斯峡谷（Box Canyon）上游等她。从博克斯峡谷的上口到怀特霍斯急流（White Horse Rapids）脚下，这段水路轮船开不过去，旅客便在那里过驳，绕过急流，从一条轮船走到另一条轮船。这地方没有电话，因此没法子通知那守候的「弗洛拉号」：「威利斯号」晚了四天，可正在赶来。

===Original===

When the *W. H. Willis* pulled into White Horse, it was learned that the *Flora* had waited three days over the limit, and had departed only a few hours before. Also, it was learned that she would tie up at Tagish Post till nine o’clock, Sunday morning. It was then four o’clock, Saturday afternoon. The pilgrims called a meeting. On board was a large Peterborough canoe, consigned to the police post at the head of Lake Bennett. They agreed to be responsible for it and to deliver it. Next, they called for volunteers. Two men were needed to make a race for the *Flora*. A score of men volunteered on the instant. Among them was Churchill, such being his nature that he volunteered before he thought of Bondell’s gripsack. When this thought came to him, he began to hope that he would not be selected; but a man who had made a name as captain of a college football eleven, as a president of an athletic club, as a dog-musher and a stampeder in the Yukon, and, moreover, who possessed such shoulders as he, had no right to avoid the honour. It was thrust upon him and upon a gigantic German, Nick Antonsen.

While a crowd of the pilgrims, the canoe on their shoulders, started on a trot over the portage, Churchill ran to his stateroom. He turned the contents of the clothes-bag on the floor and caught up the grip, with the intention of entrusting it to the man next door. Then the thought smote him that it was not his grip, and that he had no right to let it out of his possession. So he dashed ashore with it and ran up the portage changing it often from one hand to the other, and wondering if it really did not weigh more than forty pounds.

It was half-past four in the afternoon when the two men started. The current of the Thirty Mile River was so strong that rarely could they use the paddles. It was out on one bank with a towline over the shoulders, stumbling over the rocks, forcing a way through the underbrush, slipping at times and falling into the water, wading often up to the knees and waist; and then, when an insurmountable bluff was encountered, it was into the canoe, out paddles, and a wild and losing dash across the current to the other bank, in paddles, over the side, and out towline again. It was exhausting work. Antonsen toiled like the giant he was, uncomplaining, persistent, but driven to his utmost by the powerful body and indomitable brain of Churchill. They never paused for rest. It was go, go, and keep on going. A crisp wind blew down the river, freezing their hands and making it imperative, from time to time, to beat the blood back into the numbed fingers.

===Chinese===

「W. H. 威利斯号」开进怀特霍斯（White Horse）的时候，大家才得知「弗洛拉号」已经超出期限多等了三天，几小时前刚刚开走。还得知她将在塔吉什驿站（Tagish Post）一直停靠到星期天上午九点。这时是星期六下午四点。淘金客们开了个会。船上有一只大彼得伯勒独木舟（Peterborough canoe），托运给贝内特湖（Lake Bennett）湖头的骑警哨所。大家答应为它负责，负责送到。接着便征集志愿者：需要两个人去追「弗洛拉号」。一眨眼工夫，就有二十来个人报了名。丘吉尔也在其中——他就是这副天性：还没想起邦德尔的提包，手已经先举了起来。等想起这一茬，他开始盼着自己落选；可是一个人，当过大学橄榄球队的队长，当过体育俱乐部的主席，在育空赶过狗橇、闯过淘金潮，何况又生着他那样一副肩膀，就没有资格躲避这份荣誉。这荣誉不由分说，砸在了他头上，也砸在大个子德国人尼克·安东森（Nick Antonsen）头上。

一群淘金客扛起独木舟，小跑着上了转运的陆路；丘吉尔跑回自己的舱房。他把衣袋里的东西统统倒在地板上，一把抓起那只提包，本想把它托付给隔壁那一位。随即一个念头重重击中了他：这包不是他的，他没有权利让它脱出自己的掌管。于是他提着它冲上岸，沿着陆路一路往上跑，左手倒右手、右手倒左手地倒个不停，心里直犯嘀咕：这包当真没有四十磅往上？

两人动身时是下午四点半。三十英里河（Thirty Mile River）的水流太急，他们几乎使不上桨。于是上岸，纤绳挎在肩头，在乱石上磕磕绊绊，从灌木丛里硬挤出一条路，不时滑倒落水，常常蹚到膝盖、蹚到腰；待到遇上翻不过去的峭壁，就上船，出桨，朝对岸横过急流来一阵徒劳的猛冲，收桨，翻舷，再下船，出纤绳。这是熬干人的活计。安东森拿出巨人的本色埋头苦干，不吭一声，锲而不舍，可还是被丘吉尔那副强健的体魄和不屈的头脑驱到了极限。他们从不停下来歇息。就是走，走，接着走。一阵凛冽的风顺河刮下来，冻僵了他们的手，逼得他们时不时把血捶回麻木的手指里去。

===Original===

As night came on, they were compelled to trust to luck. They fell repeatedly on the untravelled banks and tore their clothing to shreds in the underbrush they could not see. Both men were badly scratched and bleeding. A dozen times, in their wild dashes from bank to bank, they struck snags and were capsized. The first time this happened, Churchill dived and groped in three feet of water for the gripsack. He lost half an hour in recovering it, and after that it was carried securely lashed to the canoe. As long as the canoe floated it was safe. Antonsen jeered at the grip, and toward morning began to curse it; but Churchill vouchsafed no explanations.

Their delays and mischances were endless. On one swift bend, around which poured a healthy young rapid, they lost two hours, making a score of attempts and capsizing twice. At this point, on both banks, were precipitous bluffs, rising out of deep water, and along which they could neither tow nor pole, while they could not gain with the paddles against the current. At each attempt they strained to the utmost with the paddles, and each time, with heads nigh to bursting from the effort, they were played out and swept back. They succeeded finally by an accident. In the swiftest current, near the end of another failure, a freak of the current sheered the canoe out of Churchill’s control and flung it against the bluff. Churchill made a blind leap at the bluff and landed in a crevice. Holding on with one hand, he held the swamped canoe with the other till Antonsen dragged himself out of the water. Then they pulled the canoe out and rested. A fresh start at this crucial point took them by. They landed on the bank above and plunged immediately ashore and into the brush with the towline.

Daylight found them far below Tagish Post. At nine o’clock Sunday morning they could hear the *Flora* whistling her departure. And when, at ten o’clock, they dragged themselves in to the Post, they could barely see the *Flora*’s smoke far to the southward. It was a pair of worn-out tatterdemalions that Captain Jones of the Mounted Police welcomed and fed, and he afterward averred that they possessed two of the most tremendous appetites he had ever observed. They lay down and slept in their wet rags by the stove. At the end of two hours Churchill got up, carried Bondell’s grip, which he had used for a pillow, down to the canoe, kicked Antonsen awake, and started in pursuit of the *Flora*.

===Chinese===

夜幕降临，他们只得听凭运气。在那些无人走过的岸上，他们一次次摔倒，衣裳被看不见的灌木丛撕成了破布条。两个人都划得伤痕累累，血一道一道的。在他们往来两岸的狂冲乱撞里，有十来回撞上暗桩，翻了船。头一回出这种事，丘吉尔潜水下去，在三英尺深的水里摸索那只提包。捞回来赔进去半个钟头，打那以后，那包便结结实实拴在独木舟上搬运。只要独木舟还浮着，它就安全。安东森起先笑话那包，到了快天亮时开始咒骂它；丘吉尔却一句解释也不给。

他们的耽搁和厄运没完没了。有一处湍急的河湾，一道年轻力壮的急流绕着它倾泻，他们在那里折损了两个钟头，试了二十来回，翻了两回船。这一段两岸尽是陡峭的绝壁，从深水里拔地而起，沿着绝壁既不能拉纤，也不能撑篙，凭桨又顶不过水流。每一回尝试，他们都把桨使到十二分，每一回都憋得脑袋几乎迸裂，力气耗尽，被水流扫回原地。末了，他们靠一场意外成了事。在最急的水流里，又一次眼看要失败的时候，一股乖张的横流把独木舟从丘吉尔的掌控里扫了出去，甩在绝壁上。丘吉尔朝着绝壁没头没脑地纵身一跃，落进一道石缝。他一只手扒住石壁，另一只手拽住灌满水的独木舟，直到安东森从水里爬出来。然后他们把舟拖出来，歇了口气。在这个要紧关口重新起程，竟一下子把他们带了过去。他们登上上游一岸，立刻扑上岸去，带着纤绳扎进灌木丛。

天亮时，他们已经在塔吉什驿站下游很远的地方。星期天上午九点，他们听得见「弗洛拉号」鸣笛启航。到十点钟他们拖着身子挪进驿站时，只勉强望得见「弗洛拉号」的烟，在南边很远的地方。骑警队（Mounted Police）的琼斯队长（Captain Jones）迎来并款待的，就是这么两个筋疲力尽的褴褛汉；他后来断言，这两位拥有他平生所见最惊人的两副好胃口。他们穿着湿透的破衣烂衫，倒在炉子边睡着了。两个钟头之后，丘吉尔爬起来，把那只一直当枕头使的邦德尔提包拎到独木舟上，一脚踢醒安东森，动身去追「弗洛拉号」。

===Original===

“There’s no telling what might happen﻿—machinery break down, or something,” was his reply to Captain Jones’s expostulations. “I’m going to catch that steamer and send her back for the boys.”

Tagish Lake was white with a fall gale that blew in their teeth. Big, swinging seas rushed upon the canoe, compelling one man to bale and leaving one man to paddle. Headway could not be made. They ran along the shallow shore and went overboard, one man ahead on the towline, the other shoving on the canoe. They fought the gale up to their waists in the icy water, often up to their necks, often over their heads and buried by the big, crested waves. There was no rest, never a moment’s pause from the cheerless, heartbreaking battle. That night, at the head of Tagish Lake, in the thick of a driving snow-squall, they overhauled the *Flora*. Antonsen fell on board, lay where he had fallen, and snored. Churchill looked like a wild man. His clothes barely clung to him. His face was iced up and swollen from the protracted effort of twenty-four hours, while his hands were so swollen that he could not close the fingers. As for his feet, it was an agony to stand upon them.

The captain of the *Flora* was loth to go back to White Horse. Churchill was persistent and imperative; the captain was stubborn. He pointed out finally that nothing was to be gained by going back, because the only ocean steamer at Dyea, the *Athenian*, was to sail on Tuesday morning, and that he could not make the back trip to White Horse and bring up the stranded pilgrims in time to make the connection.

===Chinese===

“说不准会出什么事——机器坏了，或者别的什么，”这就是他对琼斯队长劝阻的回答。“我要赶上那条船，叫她回去接弟兄们。”

塔吉什湖（Tagish Lake）上一场秋风迎面刮来，满湖白浪。一个个大浪摆荡着扑向独木舟，逼得一个人只顾舀水，只留下一个人划桨。船寸步难进。他们沿着浅岸推进，双双下了水，一个人在前头拉纤，一个在后头推船。他们在冰水里同狂风搏斗，水深及腰，常常及颈，常常没顶，被顶着一道道白冠的大浪埋住。没有休息，这场阴冷揪心的战斗连片刻的停顿也没有。当天夜里，在塔吉什湖的湖头，顶着漫天扑面的暴雪，他们追上了「弗洛拉号」。安东森一上船就栽倒，倒在哪儿就睡在哪儿，鼾声大作。丘吉尔活像个野人。衣裳勉强挂在他身上。二十四小时苦斗下来，他的脸结了冰、肿了起来，两只手肿得攥不拢手指。至于那双脚，踩上去就是一种酷刑。

「弗洛拉号」的船长不情愿回怀特霍斯去。丘吉尔步步紧逼，口气不容商量；船长偏偏执拗。末了他指出，回去毫无益处，因为迪亚（Dyea）那儿唯一的海轮「雅典人号」（*Athenian*）定于星期二早上启航，他没法赶回怀特霍斯，再把困在那儿的淘金客接上来，还赶得上衔接那班船。

===Original===

“What time does the *Athenian* sail?” Churchill demanded.

“Seven o’clock, Tuesday morning.”

“All right,” Churchill said, at the same time kicking a tattoo on the ribs of the snoring Antonsen. “You go back to White Home. We’ll go ahead and hold the *Athenian*.”

Antonsen, stupid with sleep, not yet clothed in his waking mind, was bundled into the canoe, and did not realize what had happened till he was drenched with the icy spray of a big sea, and heard Churchill snarling at him through the darkness:﻿—

“Paddle, can’t you! Do you want to be swamped?”

===Chinese===

“「雅典人号」几点开？”丘吉尔问。

“七点，星期二早上。”

“好，”丘吉尔说，同时在那打着鼾的安东森的肋骨上敲起一串连珠鼓点。“你回怀特霍斯去。我们赶到前头，把「雅典人号」扣住。”

安东森还让睡意泡得木呆呆的，神魂还没穿上醒来的衣裳，就被塞进了独木舟，直到一个大浪的冰冷水沫兜头浇下，又听见丘吉尔在黑暗里冲他咆哮，才明白出了什么事：——

“划桨，你会不会！想叫船灌沉了吗？”

===Original===

Daylight found them at Caribou Crossing, the wind dying down, and Antonsen too far gone to dip a paddle. Churchill grounded the canoe on a quiet beach, where they slept. He took the precaution of twisting his arm under the weight of his head. Every few minutes the pain of the pent circulation aroused him, whereupon he would look at his watch and twist the other arm under his head. At the end of two hours he fought with Antonsen to rouse him. Then they started. Lake Bennett, thirty miles in length, was like a millpond; but, halfway across, a gale from the south smote them and turned the water white. Hour after hour they repeated the struggle on Tagish, over the side, pulling and shoving on the canoe, up to their waists and necks, and over their heads, in the icy water; toward the last the good-natured giant played completely out. Churchill drove him mercilessly; but when he pitched forward and bade fair to drown in three feet of water, the other dragged him into the canoe. After that, Churchill fought on alone, arriving at the police post at the head of Bennett in the early afternoon. He tried to help Antonsen out of the canoe, but failed. He listened to the exhausted man’s heavy breathing, and envied him when he thought of what he himself had yet to undergo. Antonsen could lie there and sleep; but he, behind time, must go on over mighty Chilcoot and down to the sea. The real struggle lay before him, and he almost regretted the strength that resided in his frame because of the torment it could inflict upon that frame.

Churchill pulled the canoe up on the beach, seized Bondell’s grip, and started on a limping dogtrot for the police post.

“There’s a canoe down there, consigned to you from Dawson,” he hurled at the officer who answered his knock. “And there’s a man in it pretty near dead. Nothing serious; only played out. Take care of him. I’ve got to rush. Goodbye. Want to catch the *Athenian*.”

===Chinese===

天亮时，他们到了卡里布克罗辛（Caribou Crossing），风势渐息，安东森也垮得连一桨都插不动了。丘吉尔把独木舟搁上一处僻静的湖滩，两人就在那儿睡了。他防了一手，把一条胳膊拧过来垫在头底下，让脑袋的分量压着它。每隔几分钟，血脉壅塞的胀痛就把他弄醒，他便看看表，换另一条胳膊垫到头下。两个钟头一到，他跟安东森纠缠了一场，才把他弄醒。然后他们动身了。贝内特湖长三十英里，平静得像口磨坊水池；可是刚过中流，一阵南来的大风劈头打下，把湖水打成了白色。一个钟头又一个钟头，他们重演塔吉什湖上的苦斗：翻舷下水，又拉又推，在冰水里没腰、没颈、没顶；到末了，那好脾气的巨人彻底垮了。丘吉尔毫不留情地驱赶他；可等他一个前扑，眼看要淹死在三英尺深的水里，又把他拖进了舟中。打那以后，丘吉尔独自苦战，午后不久，赶到了贝内特湖头的骑警哨所。他想把安东森扶下船，没扶动。他听着那精疲力竭的人沉重的喘息，想到自己前头还要受的罪，竟羡慕起他来。安东森可以躺在那儿睡；他却不行——他误了时辰，还必须翻过巍峨的奇尔库特山（Chilcoot），一路下到海边。真正的苦斗还在前头；他几乎悔恨自己这副筋骨里住着的那股力气，因为它能加给这副筋骨的折磨太大了。

丘吉尔把独木舟拖上湖滩，抓起邦德尔的提包，一瘸一拐地小跑着奔骑警哨所去。

“下头有一条独木舟，是道森托运给你们的，”他冲着应门的警员甩下这话。“舟里还有个人，快死了。不碍事，只是累垮了。照看一下他。我得赶路。再见。要去追「雅典人号」。”

===Original===

A mile portage connected Lake Bennett and Lake Linderman, and his last words he flung back after him as he resumed the trot. It was a very painful trot, but he clenched his teeth and kept on, forgetting his pain most of the time in the fervent heat with which he regarded the gripsack. It was a severe handicap. He swung it from one hand to the other, and back again. He tucked it under his arm. He threw one hand over the opposite shoulder, and the bag bumped and pounded on his back as he ran along. He could scarcely hold it in his bruised and swollen fingers, and several times he dropped it. Once, in changing from one hand to the other, it escaped his clutch and fell in front of him, tripped him up, and threw him violently to the ground.

At the far end of the portage he bought an old set of pack-straps for a dollar, and in them he swung the grip. Also, he chartered a launch to run him the six miles to the upper end of Lake Linderman, where he arrived at four in the afternoon. The *Athenian* was to sail from Dyea next morning at seven. Dyea was twenty-eight miles away, and between towered Chilcoot. He sat down to adjust his footgear for the long climb, and woke up. He had dozed the instant he sat down, though he had not slept thirty seconds. He was afraid his next doze might be longer, so he finished fixing his footgear standing up. Even then he was overpowered for a fleeting moment. He experienced the flash of unconsciousness; becoming aware of it, in midair, as his relaxed body was sinking to the ground and as he caught himself together, he stiffened his muscles with a spasmodic wrench, and escaped the fall. The sudden jerk back to consciousness left him sick and trembling. He beat his head with the heel of his hand, knocking wakefulness into the numbed brain.

Jack Burns’s pack-train was starting back light for Crater Lake, and Churchill was invited to a mule. Burns wanted to put the gripsack on another animal, but Churchill held on to it, carrying it on his saddle-pommel. But he dozed, and the grip persisted in dropping off the pommel, one side or the other, each time wakening him with a sickening start. Then, in the early darkness, Churchill’s mule brushed him against a projecting branch that laid his cheek open. To cap it, the mule blundered off the trail and fell, throwing rider and gripsack out upon the rocks. After that, Churchill walked, or stumbled rather, over the apology for a trail, leading the mule. Stray and awful odours, drifting from each side of the trail, told of the horses that had died in the rush for gold. But he did not mind. He was too sleepy. By the time Long Lake was reached, however, he had recovered from his sleepiness; and at Deep Lake he resigned the gripsack to Burns. But thereafter, by the light of the dim stars, he kept his eyes on Burns. There were not going to be any accidents with that bag.

===Chinese===

一条一英里的转运陆路连着贝内特湖和林德曼湖（Lake Linderman），他重新撒开小跑的时候，最后这几句话是甩在身后扔回去的。这小跑苦不堪言，可他咬紧牙关不停步，大半时候连疼都忘了——他全副心神都火辣辣地盯在那只提包上。那是个不轻的累赘。他把它从一只手倒到另一只手，又倒回来。他把它夹在腋下。他把一只手搭过对面的肩，让那包在他跑动时磕磕碰碰地砸在背上。他那青肿的指头几乎攥不住它，好几次脱了手。有一回换手，它挣脱了他的掌握，掉在他身前，绊了他一跤，把他狠狠掼在地上。

到了转运陆路的那一头，他花一块钱买下一副旧的背货带，把提包吊在带上背着。他还雇了一条小汽艇，送他六英里水路，到林德曼湖的上端，下午四点到了那里。「雅典人号」明早七点要从迪亚启航。迪亚远在二十八英里之外，中间矗立着奇尔库特山。他坐下来整顿鞋袜，好应付漫长的爬坡——一坐下就醒了。原来他刚坐下就打起了盹，虽然统共没睡上三十秒。他怕下一个盹会更长，便站着把鞋袜整顿完。饶是这样，还是有一瞬间被击倒了。他体验了那道失去知觉的闪光；等他觉察过来，人已在半空，松弛的身子正往地上沉，他把全身猛地一收一绷，才没有摔下去。这一下猛地拽回知觉，弄得他又恶心又发抖。他用手掌根敲自己的脑袋，把清醒敲进麻木的脑子里去。

杰克·伯恩斯（Jack Burns）的驮队正空着驮子返回克雷特湖（Crater Lake），丘吉尔受邀分得一头骡子骑。伯恩斯想把提包放到另一头牲口上，丘吉尔不撒手，把它挂在鞍头上驮着。可他一路打盹，那包偏一个劲儿地从鞍头往这边滑、往那边滑，每回都叫他一阵反胃地惊醒。接着，天刚擦黑，丘吉尔的骡子把他往一根横出的树枝上蹭，把他的脸颊豁开了一道口子。更糟的是，骡子又失蹄栽下道，摔了一跤，把骑的人和提包一齐甩在石头上。打那以后，丘吉尔牵着骡子，在那条勉强算是路的东西上走——不如说是深一脚浅一脚地蹭。道旁两侧不时飘来一阵阵骇人的恶臭，那是淘金狂潮中倒毙的马匹发出的。可他不在乎。他太困了。不过，到了长湖（Long Lake），他的睡意总算过去了；到了深湖（Deep Lake），他把提包交给了伯恩斯。可是从此以后，他借着幽暗的星光，眼睛就没离开过伯恩斯。那只包，绝不容出半点岔子。

===Original===

At Crater Lake, the pack-train went into camp, and Churchill, slinging the grip on his back, started the steep climb for the summit. For the first time, on that precipitous wall, he realized how tired he was. He crept and crawled like a crab, burdened by the weight of his limbs. A distinct and painful effort of will was required each time he lifted a foot. An hallucination came to him that he was shod with lead, like a deep-sea diver, and it was all he could do to resist the desire to reach down and feel the lead. As for Bondell’s gripsack, it was inconceivable that forty pounds could weigh so much. It pressed him down like a mountain, and he looked back with unbelief to the year before, when he had climbed that same pass with a hundred and fifty pounds on his back. If those loads had weighed a hundred and fifty pounds, then Bondell’s grip weighed five hundred.

The first rise of the divide from Crater Lake was across a small glacier. Here was a well-defined trail. But above the glacier, which was also above timberline, was naught but a chaos of naked rock and enormous boulders. There was no way of seeing the trail in the darkness, and he blundered on, paying thrice the ordinary exertion for all that he accomplished. He won the summit in the thick of howling wind and driving snow, providentially stumbling upon a small, deserted tent, into which he crawled. There he found and bolted some ancient fried potatoes and half a dozen raw eggs.

When the snow ceased and the wind eased down, he began the almost impossible descent. There was no trail, and he stumbled and blundered, often finding himself, at the last moment, on the edge of rocky walls and steep slopes the depth of which he had no way of judging. Part way down, the stars clouded over again, and in the consequent obscurity he slipped and rolled and slid for a hundred feet, landing bruised and bleeding on the bottom of a large shallow hole. From all about him arose the stench of dead horses. The hole was handy to the trail, and the packers had made a practice of tumbling into it their broken and dying animals. The stench overpowered him, making him deadly sick, and as in a nightmare he scrambled out. Halfway up, he recollected Bondell’s gripsack. It had fallen into the hole with him; the pack-strap had evidently broken, and he had forgotten it. Back he went into the pestilential charnel-pit, where he crawled around on hands and knees and groped for half an hour. Altogether he encountered and counted seventeen dead horses (and one horse still alive that he shot with his revolver) before he found Bondell’s grip. Looking back upon a life that had not been without valour and achievement, he unhesitatingly declared to himself that this return after the grip was the most heroic act he had ever performed. So heroic was it that he was twice on the verge of fainting before he crawled out of the hole.

===Chinese===

到了克雷特湖，驮队扎下营，丘吉尔把提包往背上一背，开始朝山顶陡峭地攀去。在这面陡壁上，他头一回认清了自己有多疲惫。他像只螃蟹似的爬着挪着，四肢的分量都成了负担。每抬一次脚，都得下一道分明而痛楚的决心。一阵幻觉袭来：他脚上穿着铅靴，像个深海潜水员，他费了好大的劲才忍住伸手下去摸摸那铅的欲望。至于邦德尔的提包，四十磅竟能有这般重法，简直不可思议。它像一座山压着他；他不敢相信地回想头一年的光景——那时他背着一百五十磅，翻过这同一道山口。倘若那些驮子重一百五十磅，那么邦德尔的提包就重五百磅。

从克雷特湖上分水岭的头一道坡，要横穿一座小冰川。这里有一条清晰的道。可冰川以上，也就是林木线以上，就只剩赤裸的岩石和巨砾的一片混沌。黑暗里没法辨认路径，他乱闯着往前走，每前进一步都付出三倍的气力。他在狂风怒号、飞雪扑面中登上了山顶，天可怜见，一头撞见一顶无人的小帐篷，便爬了进去。他在里面找到并吞下了几块陈年的煎土豆，和半打生鸡蛋。

雪住了，风也缓了，他开始这趟几乎不可能的下山。没有路，他跌跌撞撞、乱冲乱闯，常常在最后一刻才发现，自己正站在石壁和陡坡的边上，深浅全无从判断。下到半途，星星又被云遮没，在随之而来的昏黑里，他脚下一滑，连滚带溜跌了一百英尺，带着一身淤伤和血，落在一个大浅坑的坑底。四面八方升腾起死马的恶臭。这坑就在道旁边，赶驮子的人惯常把摔残的、垂死的牲口扔进去。恶臭压倒了他，熏得他五内翻腾，他像在噩梦里似的拼命爬了出去。爬到半坡，他想起了邦德尔的提包。它是跟他一道跌进坑里的；背带显然断了，而他竟把它忘了。他折回那瘟疫般的马尸坑，在里头手脚并用地爬来爬去，摸索了半个钟头。他先后遭遇、并数出了十七匹死马（还有一匹没断气的，他用左轮枪给了它一枪），才找到邦德尔的提包。回首平生——那也并非没有豪勇与建树——他毫不迟疑地对自己断言：这趟回身去取提包，是他此生干过的最英勇的壮举。这壮举英勇到什么地步呢——还没爬出坑，他就两回险些晕了过去。

===Original===

By the time he had descended to the Scales, the steep pitch of Chilcoot was past, and the way became easier. Not that it was an easy way, however, in the best of places; but it became a really possible trail, along which he could have made good time if he had not been worn out, if he had had light with which to pick his steps, and if it had not been for Bondell’s gripsack. To him, in his exhausted condition, it was the last straw. Having barely strength to carry himself along, the additional weight of the grip was sufficient to throw him nearly every time he tripped or stumbled. And when he escaped tripping, branches reached out in the darkness, hooked the grip between his shoulders, and held him back.

His mind was made up that if he missed the *Athenian* it would be the fault of the gripsack. In fact, only two things remained in his consciousness﻿—Bondell’s grip and the steamer. He knew only those two things, and they became identified, in a way, with some stern mission upon which he had journeyed and toiled for centuries. He walked and struggled on as in a dream. As part of the dream was his arrival at Sheep Camp. He stumbled into a saloon, slid his shoulders out of the straps, and started to deposit the grip at his feet. But it slipped from his fingers and struck the floor with a heavy thud that was not unnoticed by two men who were just leaving. Churchill drank a glass of whisky, told the barkeeper to call him in ten minutes, and sat down, his feet on the grip, his head on his knees.

So badly did his misused body stiffen, that when he was called it required another ten minutes and a second glass of whisky to unbend his joints and limber up the muscles.

===Chinese===

下到秤台（the Scales）时，奇尔库特最陡的一段坡已经过去，路好走些了。倒不是说这条路就是在顶好的地段也好走；而是它总算成了一条真正可行的道，若不是他已筋疲力尽，若不是他没有亮光好挑拣落脚处，若不是为了邦德尔那只提包，他本可以在这条道上赶出好脚程的。在他眼下这耗竭的境地，那包就是压垮骆驼的最后一根稻草。他连拖着自己走的力气都勉强，提包这一点额外的分量，就足以在他每次绊脚、踉跄的时候把他掼倒。就算躲过了绊脚，黑暗里还有枝条伸出来，钩住他两肩之间的提包，把他往回拽。

他打定了主意：要是误了「雅典人号」，那就是提包的罪过。实际上，他的意识里只剩下两样东西——邦德尔的提包，和那条轮船。他只知道这两样东西，而在某种意义上，它们已经同一桩庄严的使命合为一体，为着这使命，他已经跋涉劳苦了几个世纪。他像在梦里一样走着、挣扎着。这梦的一部分，是他到了羊营（Sheep Camp）。他跌跌撞撞进了一家酒馆，肩膀从背带里褪出来，正要把提包放在脚边。可它从手指间滑脱，砰的一声闷响砸在地板上，这声响没有逃过两个正要离去的人。丘吉尔灌下一杯威士忌，嘱咐酒保十分钟后叫醒他，坐了下来，脚踩着提包，头伏在膝上。

他那被作践透了的身子僵得那样厉害，以至被叫醒之后，还得再花上十分钟、再灌一杯威士忌，才把关节掰开、把肌肉活动起来。

===Original===

“Hey not that way!” the barkeeper shouted, and then went after him and started him through the darkness toward Canyon City. Some little husk of inner consciousness told Churchill that the direction was right, and, still as in a dream, he took the canyon trail. He did not know what warned him, but after what seemed several centuries of travelling, he sensed danger and drew his revolver. Still in the dream, he saw two men step out and heard them halt him. His revolver went off four times, and he saw the flashes and heard the explosions of their revolvers. Also, he was aware that he had been hit in the thigh. He saw one man go down, and, as the other came for him, he smashed him a straight blow with the heavy revolver full in the face. Then he turned and ran. He came from the dream shortly afterward, to find himself plunging down the trail at a limping lope. His first thought was for the gripsack. It was still on his back. He was convinced that what had happened was a dream till he felt for his revolver and found it gone. Next he became aware of a sharp stinging of his thigh, and after investigating, he found his hand warm with blood. It was a superficial wound, but it was incontestable. He became wider awake, and kept up the lumbering run to Canyon City.

He found a man, with a team of horses and a wagon, who got out of bed and harnessed up for twenty dollars. Churchill crawled in on the wagon-bed and slept, the gripsack still on his back. It was a rough ride, over water-washed boulders down the Dyea Valley; but he roused only when the wagon hit the highest places. Any altitude of his body above the wagon-bed of less than a foot did not faze him. The last mile was smooth going, and he slept soundly.

He came to in the grey dawn, the driver shaking him savagely and howling into his ear that the *Athenian* was gone. Churchill looked blankly at the deserted harbour.

===Chinese===

“嘿，不是那边！”酒保喊道，接着又追上他，把他打发进黑暗里，往峡谷城（Canyon City）去。内心意识的一点残壳告诉丘吉尔，方向没有错，于是他仍旧像在梦里，踏上了峡谷小道。他不知道是什么警示了他，可是走了仿佛有几个世纪之后，他嗅出了危险，拔出左轮枪。还在梦里，他看见两个人闪身出来，听见他们喝令他站住。他的左轮枪响了四下，他看见对面枪口的火光，听见他们左轮枪的炸响。他还觉出自己大腿上挨了一枪。他看见一个人倒了下去；另一个朝他扑来时，他抡起那把沉重的左轮枪，照那张脸正正地砸了一记直拳。然后他转身跑了。不久，他从梦里醒来，发现自己正一瘸一拐、大步流星地顺着道往山下冲。他头一个念头是提包。它还在背上。他起先认定方才发生的一切是场梦，直到伸手去摸左轮枪，才发现枪没了。接着他觉出大腿上一阵尖锐的刺痛，一探查，摸到一手温热的血。伤得不深，却无可置疑。他彻底醒了过来，迈着沉重蹒跚的步子，一路跑到了峡谷城。

他找到一个人，有一队马和一辆车，那人为了二十块钱下了床，套好了马。丘吉尔爬进车斗里睡觉，提包还背在背上。这一路颠得厉害，顺着迪亚山谷碾过被水冲刷的圆石；可只有车轮撞上最高的坎时，他才醒一醒。他的身子只要不弹离车斗一英尺以上，就惊不动他。最后一英里路平平坦坦，他睡得死沉。

灰蒙蒙的黎明里他醒了过来，车夫正狠命地摇他，对着他的耳朵嚎：「雅典人号」开走了。丘吉尔木然望着空荡荡的港口。

===Original===

“There’s a smoke over at Skaguay,” the man said.

Churchill’s eyes were too swollen to see that far, but he said: “It’s she. Get me a boat.”

The driver was obliging and found a skiff, and a man to row it for ten dollars, payment in advance. Churchill paid, and was helped into the skiff. It was beyond him to get in by himself. It was six miles to Skaguay, and he had a blissful thought of sleeping those six miles. But the man did not know how to row, and Churchill took the oars and toiled for a few more centuries. He never knew six longer and more excruciating miles. A snappy little breeze blew up the inlet and held him back. He had a gone feeling at the pit of the stomach, and suffered from faintness and numbness. At his command, the man took the bailer and threw salt water into his face.

The *Athenian*’s anchor was up-and-down when they came alongside, and Churchill was at the end of his last remnant of strength.

===Chinese===

“斯卡圭（Skaguay）那边有一股烟。”那人说。

丘吉尔的眼睛肿得望不了那么远，可他还是说：“是她。给我弄条船来。”

车夫热心肠，找来一条小划子，还找来一个划船的人，要价十块，先付。丘吉尔付了钱，让人搀进了划子。他自己一个人已经上不去船了。到斯卡圭有六英里，他美滋滋地盘算着，这六英里可以在睡梦里过去。可那人不会划船，丘吉尔接过桨来，又苦熬了几个世纪。他这辈子还没见识过比这更长、更折磨人的六英里。一阵脆生生的小风顺着海湾吹上来，拽住他不放。他心口底下虚得发空，一阵阵发晕、发麻。他一声令下，那人操起水瓢，舀起咸水泼在他脸上。

他们靠拢船舷的时候，「雅典人号」的锚链已经吊得笔直，眼看就要起锚，而丘吉尔也把最后一丝残余的力气使到了头。

===Original===

“Stop her! Stop her!” he shouted hoarsely.

“Important message! Stop her!”

Then he dropped his chin on his chest and slept. When half a dozen men started to carry him up the gangplank, he awoke, reached for the grip, and clung to it like a drowning man.

On deck he became a centre of horror and curiosity. The clothing in which he had left White Horse was represented by a few rags, and he was as frayed as his clothing. He had travelled for fifty-five hours at the top notch of endurance. He had slept six hours in that time, and he was twenty pounds lighter than when he started. Face and hands and body were scratched and bruised, and he could scarcely see. He tried to stand up, but failed, sprawling out on the deck, hanging on to the gripsack, and delivering his message.

“Now, put me to bed,” he finished; “I’ll eat when I wake up.”

===Chinese===

“拦住她！拦住她！”他嘶哑地喊。

“要紧的口信！拦住她！”

说完，他下巴一垂抵在胸口，睡着了。六个人动手把他抬上跳板时，他醒了，伸手就去抓提包，像溺水的人一样死死攥住不放。

到了甲板上，他成了惊骇与好奇的一个中心。他从怀特霍斯动身时穿的那身衣裳，如今只剩几条破布；他本人也和那身衣裳一样褴褛。他以耐力的极限赶了五十五个钟头的路。这中间他睡了六个钟头，人比动身时轻了二十磅。脸、手和周身尽是划伤和淤伤，眼睛几乎睁不开。他想站起来，没成，摊开在甲板上，攥住提包不放，说出了他的口信。

“好了，把我弄上床去吧，”末了他说；“醒了再吃。”

===Original===

They did him honour, carrying him down in his rags and dirt and depositing him and Bondell’s grip in the bridal chamber, which was the biggest and most luxurious stateroom in the ship. Twice he slept the clock around, and he had bathed and shaved and eaten and was leaning over the rail smoking a cigar when the two hundred pilgrims from White Horse came alongside.

By the time the *Athenian* arrived in Seattle, Churchill had fully recuperated, and he went ashore with Bondell’s grip in his hand. He felt proud of that grip. To him it stood for achievement and integrity and trust. “I’ve delivered the goods,” was the way he expressed these various high terms to himself. It was early in the evening, and he went straight to Bondell’s home. Louis Bondell was glad to see him, shaking hands with both hands at the same time and dragging him into the house.

“Oh, thanks, old man; it was good of you to bring it out,” Bondell said when he received the gripsack.

He tossed it carelessly upon a couch, and Churchill noted with an appreciative eye the rebound of its weight from the springs. Bondell was volleying him with questions.

===Chinese===

大家给了他体面，就让他那样一身破烂污垢，把他抬了下去，连他带邦德尔的提包，一起安置在新房里——那是全船最大、最豪华的特等舱。他两度一觉睡足了一整个昼夜，然后洗了澡，刮了脸，吃了饭，正倚着栏杆抽雪茄，从怀特霍斯来的那两百名淘金客靠上了船。

「雅典人号」抵达西雅图（Seattle）的时候，丘吉尔已经完全复原，他手里提着邦德尔的提包下了船。他为那只提包感到自豪。在他心里，它代表着功业、信义，和所托。“我把货送到了。”——他就是用这一句话，把这些种种崇高的字眼对自己说了一遍。天刚擦黑，他径直去了邦德尔家。路易斯·邦德尔见到他很高兴，两只手一齐握上来，把他拽进屋里。

“哎呀，多谢，老伙计；难为你把它带出来了。”邦德尔接过提包时说。

他随手把它往沙发上一丢，丘吉尔用行家的眼光，注意到弹簧承住那分量时往下一沉、又弹了回来。邦德尔连珠炮似的朝他发问。

===Original===

“How did you make out? How’re the boys? What became of Bill Smithers? Is Del Bishop still with Pierce? Did he sell my dogs? How did Sulphur Bottom show up? You’re looking fine. What steamer did you come out on?”

To all of which Churchill gave answer, till half an hour had gone by and the first lull in the conversation had arrived.

“Hadn’t you better take a look at it?” he suggested, nodding his head at the gripsack.

“Oh, it’s all right,” Bondell answered. “Did Mitchell’s dump turn out as much as he expected?”

“I think you’d better look at it,” Churchill insisted. “When I deliver a thing, I want to be satisfied that it’s all right. There’s always the chance that somebody might have got into it when I was asleep, or something.”

===Chinese===

“你们过得怎么样？弟兄们都好吗？比尔·史密瑟斯（Bill Smithers）后来怎么了？德尔·毕晓普（Del Bishop）还跟着皮尔斯（Pierce）吗？他把我的狗卖了吗？硫磺滩（Sulphur Bottom）那边出息怎么样？你气色好得很。你是坐哪条船出来的？”

这一连串的问题，丘吉尔一一作了答，直到半个钟头过去，谈话头一次出现了间歇。

“你最好还是看它一眼吧？”他朝提包点了点头，提议道。

“哦，没事的。”邦德尔答道。“米切尔（Mitchell）那堆囤的矿砂，淘出的金子赶得上他指望的数吗？”

“我看你还是看一眼的好。”丘吉尔坚持道。“我送交一样东西，就要确知它安然无恙。保不齐我睡着的时候，有什么人翻过它，或者出点别的什么事。”

===Original===

“It’s nothing important, old man,” Bondell answered, with a laugh.

“Nothing important,” Churchill echoed in a faint, small voice. Then he spoke with decision: “Louis, what’s in that bag? I want to know.”

Louis looked at him curiously, then left the room and returned with a bunch of keys. He inserted his hand and drew out a heavy Colt’s revolver. Next came out a few boxes of ammunition for the revolver and several boxes of Winchester cartridges.

Churchill took the gripsack and looked into it. Then he turned it upside down and shook it gently.

===Chinese===

“里面没什么要紧的东西，老伙计。”邦德尔笑着答道。

“没什么要紧的东西。”丘吉尔用一种微弱细小的声音跟着念了一遍。随后他斩钉截铁地说：“路易斯，那只包里是什么？我要知道。”

路易斯好奇地打量了他一阵，然后走出房间，拿回来一串钥匙。他把手伸进包里，掏出一把沉甸甸的柯尔特左轮枪（Colt）。接着掏出来的，是几盒配这枪的子弹，还有几盒温彻斯特连发枪（Winchester）的枪弹。

丘吉尔拿过提包，朝里面看了看。然后把它倒过来，轻轻摇了摇。

===Original===

“The gun’s all rusted,” Bondell said. “Must have been out in the rain.”

“Yes,” Churchill answered. “Too bad it got wet. I guess I was a bit careless.”

He got up and went outside. Ten minutes later Louis Bondell went out and found him on the steps, sitting down, elbows on knees and chin on hands, gazing steadfastly out into the darkness.

===Chinese===

“枪全长锈了。”邦德尔说。“一定是淋过雨了。”

“是啊。”丘吉尔答道。“淋湿了，可惜。想来是我有点儿大意。”

他站起身，走了出去。十分钟以后，路易斯·邦德尔也走了出去，只见他坐在台阶上，肘抵着膝，下巴搁在手上，目不转睛地凝望着外面的黑暗。
